To Advertisers.
We devote all the apace we ean possibly spare
to advertisers in this morning** paper, and
Uaose who find that their advertisements are left
oat will pardon U to the necessity of riving a
ixlr portion ofonreolomna the reading pub
lic. The demand for intelligence is not only in
satiable, hat it is absolutely necessary to the
welfare of the people and the city that much of
(hat which we pnbllah should go to the public.
We divide our space between the reader and the
advertiser, and that Is the best wo ean do for
both elates. The advertiser will understand
that the earlier in the day ho gives us what he
wishes to have published, and the smaller the
rpace into which he condenses it, the more
likely it Is to find a place in our columns.
FOB MA.TOB, HEXBY GREECE-
BAtrai.
There seems to be a general feeling in 1
tile several divisions of the city in favor of i
the election of Hexry Greenebaum for
Mayor. Mr. Greenebaum baa proved bim-
self.one of tbe most systematic and tbor-
ougb-golng workers In the whole city since
the fire broke out. His bead has been
level all tbe time, and bis bands busy with
well-directed efforts to bring order out of
confusion, and fortitude out of consterna
tion. He is a resident of the West Dlvi*
cion, where a majority of the voters arc
now domiciled. He is a German by birth,
but has resided in Chicago longer than
nineteen-twentieths of our native-born pop
ulation. His character is above suspicion
in all respects. His former service in the
Board of Aldermen has made him familiar
with public business. He is one of the
few men fit to be entrusted with the office
of Mayor at a junction like the present.
We commend Mr. Greenebaum earnest
ly to the consideration of our stricken
people, believing him to bo equal to the
emergency. It is unnecessary to add that
be has not been consulted in advance in
reference to his possible candidacy, but it
is believed that no man who has a heart in
his breast will refuse to serve his fellow
citizens now, in any capacity, where they
think he can serve them best.
♦the action of the state.
The people of Chicago have been placed
under a debt of everlasting gratitude to
the State of Illinois. In all States there
i 8 more or less ill-feeling on the part of
smaller cities and the agricultural districts
toward the metropolis ofthe State. Boston,
New York, Philadelphia, Baltimore, and
each of the other large cities, have had
to contend with prejudices which naturally
sprung up against any community exer
cising an extraordinary proportion of
political power, and whose interests are
more or less peculiar and concentrated.
Chicago has been no exception to the gen
oral rule. But the terrible calamity with
which we have been visited has
dismantled and thrown down every
prejudice. The disaster has been
contemplated as one to the State at large,
and from all parts of Illinois tho pee pie
have sent up representatives to accept on
their behalf so much of the heavy loss as
can be constitutionally transferred to them.
The voice of party and of sectionalism
was silenced. In the language of a mem*
ber from a far-off county, Chicago in ashes
was too powerful for opposition. The
people of the State have not only shared
with ns their bread, their provisions, their
dotbing, and their money, hot they have
now, through the Legislature, promptly,
and unanimously, passed all the relief
bills which it was possible for them to
pass, and which even prostrate Chicago
could ask. The bill by which the State
refunded ns our expenditure on the canal
was of immense relief, and its passage
at any other time would have been impos
sible. There were strong economic objec
tions honestly entertained by many mem'
bers; but tbe generous hearts of the people
have risen above all other considerations,
and into onr lap have been poured every
dollar that the State could properly give.
To onr fellow citizens of Illinois, in behalf
of their stricken city, we return thanks—
the thanks which come from hearts that
can only be overcome by kindness. In tbe
future, when Chicago shall have been re
stored to her true power and glory, it will
be her province to do whatever may be
within ber reach to enrich, honor, and cn
oble the great State of Illinois and her
generous-hearted people.
MAYOR MASOIV—THE FIRE.
Mayor Mason has been accused by the
Chicago Times of inefficiency and indiffer
ence during the progress of the fire. We
are authoritatively informed that on Mon
day morning, when the fire crossed the
South Branch of the river, Maj*or Mason,
accompanied by his two sons and H. G.
Miller Esq., left his house and proceeded
directly to his office in the Court House
building. He sent his sons to find out
about the fire and report to him. He went
to police headquarters and telegraphed to
Milwaukee, Joliet, Springfield, and Detroit
for fire engines, and gave an order to use
powder to olow up buildings under the di
rection of the Fire Marshal When the roof
of the old Court House took fire, be sent a
written order to the Sheriff* to release th®
prisoners in the jail. He remained in the
bnilding until some time after the roof of
the old building had fallen in. He then
tried to reach the lake shore,
but could not get through Randolph, Lake,
or Water streets; he crossed Clark street
bridge in company with ex-Mayor Ram
sey, and went some blocks north, when ho
turned to Rush street bridge, and by the
bridge on that street got to Michigan ave-
nne. He then personally directed the
"tSßriq/; down and blowing up of buildings
on Wab&sh avenue and Harrison streets,
by which the fire was ultimately checked
in that direction. The progress of the
fire, after it reached Madison etrcct, and
had attacked the Chamber of Commerce,
the Union Bank Block, and the Court
House, was rapid in tbo extreme. The
heat was intense, and buildings were on
fire at a dozen points in advaccc of those
places. It was not in the -power of human
agencies to pat out the fire anywhere in
the line of that fierce wind. It was not
Mayor Mason's fault that it was not stop
ped, nor was it under his administration
that the wooden roof was put on tho pump*
ing works.

their families. Anything done to re-es

much greater relief than that. afforded by p

THE CHICAGO BELIEF SOCIETY.
Tho great calamity which bos overtaken
Chicago has developed the executive abil
ity of the Chicago Relief Society in the
most remarkable and gratifying manner.
Prior to the fire it bad always been effi-
I dent, but Us operations were compara
tively limited, and Us plan of relief simple
and easily managed. Almost at a mo*
ment’a notice the conflagration extended
its field o( labor. Thousands ot homeless
women and children and suffering men,
destitute of provisions, shelter, and cloth
ing, were suddenly thrown, upon Us char-
Ity, instead of the few hundreds who had
previously relied upon it Their appeal
1 for relief was instant and urgent. Not
only must provision bo made for them
daring the coming winter, but their pres
ent necessities must also be met It was
!an emergency which had never been con
templated, or even supposed to be proba-1
blc; but the society instantly met the
emergency without quailing, although!
nearly every member of it was more or]
less concerned with his own personal losses
and troubles, and proved itself equal to it
I In meeting this great question of relief
I the society found an ample and indispen
sable auxiliary in the unexampled tide of
contributions which flowed- hitherward
from every city in the country. There
was danger, however, that in this sponta
neous and impulsive oatpouring, the city
would be inundated and the contributions
wasted. Tho society bent its first ener- j
gies to the avoidance of this danger. It
districted tho city, established storehouses,
and appointed competent men to receive, j
arrange, and care for the supplies. Having
satisfactorily established its base of gen*
eral operations, it then turned its atten
tion to the details of the work before it
Having been officially invested with su
i preme power in the matter of relief, and
having regulated the auxiliary societies
of churches and other institutions, it com-
I mcnccd the herculean task of examining
the individual applicants who presented
[themselves. Their names were registered,
and their actual condition ascertained* If
able-bodied men presented themselves, and
I were willing to work, employment was]
provided for them; if they were unwilling
to work, they were turned away as un
worthy of aid, and without ceremony.
Those men who could not work, and women
and children, were supplied with immedi
ate aid. The hungry found food, the
naked clothes, and the homeless, shelter.
The society now has its plans for the
present fully matured, and its machinery
in perfect working order. No person need
have apprehensions of suffering who is
worthy to receive relief Its next great
duty is to forestall suffering during the
coming and it has entered upon
these duties with the same energy of pur
pose and remarkable executive bility
that characterized it daring the raemora*
ble fire week. It has already creeled one
hundred and thirty-five comfortable homes
for the homeless, and is making such ar
rangements for fuel, food, and clothing,
that none shall go cold or hungry. The
performance of the past is a guarantee
j for tho future. It should be tho grateful
task of every good citizen to aid the eoci
ety by relieving individual suffering in his
own neighborhood, and thus enable it to
concentrate its efforts where it can do the
most good. Chicago has faith in the fu
ture,—has hope growing out of the ruins
of her past; but tho greatest of these is
charity;" and among all the noble deeds
and shining examples, and self-sacrifice
and devotion to humanity developed by
the great calamity, none stand clearer or
brighter than the work of tho Chicago Re
'ief Society.
VAGABONDISM
To avoid impositions, and the danger of
bestowing charity upon unworthy people,
will bo a matter of extreme difficulty. At
nearly every church In the West Division,
yesterday, great, lazy, able-bodied hulks ot
men were sitting and lounging about the
churches, smoking their pipes, and waiting
for their turn to be helped. These do*
nothings are taking the bread out of the
months of deserving, suffering women and
children. There is work onough for these
men to do of some sort, and no relief
should be afforded them whatever ontll
they show that they can get nothing
to do. If a man won’t work when he
can work, let him go hungry. Just as
long as this class arc encouraged by relief,
just so long will they remain idle, and their
numbers will increase by the demoraliza
tion which their example will have upon
others who are not at work. Let these vag
abonds once know that there is no help for
them, except in honest labor,and they will
go to work and swell the ranks of the Tin
Kettle Brigade, whose processions at
morning and evening from the South Side
to the West Side, and from the North Side
to the Sooth Side, give a cheerful, encour
aging aspect to the cheerless ruins. But
the .Relief Society understand this thing
better than we do. They arc weeding out
the vagabonds with systematic rapidity.
Another question is arising in the social
economy which always has made trouble*
and now presents new phases of difficulty.
The question turns upon the relations of
servant girls and mistresses. Many ser
vants are refusing to work at all, and sub
sisting upon charity. This waste of relief,
as iu the case of lazy male laborers, should
be checked at once. Others are demand
ing higher wages, at a time when house*
keepers feel it necessary to retrench in
every direction. There is a glut of ser
vant girls on the market, to .speak in com
mercial phrase. Hundreds are out of em
ployment, and those in service, with a stu
pidity which seems unaccountable, de
mand an increase of pay, trusting that
those out of work will adhere to
their position. For once there is a
spirit on the part of housekeepers to
resist this demand, and a movement is
already on foot to bring about a consulta
tion between prominent ladies of the West
and South Divisions, in which they may
fix npon some definite plan, and work to*
gether for the common good. They are
determined to be no longer overridden by
their former tyrants, and will give their
servants to understand that they, os well
as mistresses, most economize and re*
trench. In view of the coming winter’
with its severities, it will be the part of
wisdom for servants to accept the now
situation, and to go to work at wages which
shall bear a just relation to tbe new basis
of domestic expenses. Acting in any
Other manner they, "and they only, must
suffer. a
And now, as there is work enough to do,
let every man go to work. There is no
surer reminder of trouble than Idleness
no easier way of forgetting trouble than
employment
A great improvement was apparent in
grain circles yesterday. Since the fire the
produce markets have been verywcaki
owing to a universal desire to realize oh
property held here, for the double reason
that money was scarce, and insurance con*
sldered to be doubtful. Hence prices of
grain fell with a. “looseness.” Yesterday
it seemed os if all the weak holders bad
sold out, and prices rallied. There is now
reason to ,hope that our produce
markets, which are so intimately identi.
fied with the commercial prosperity of the
city, will be more healthy daring the com
ing winter than usual Grain is being
sent Eastward at the rate of 400,000 bush
els per day, and the shipments are so far
in excess of the receipts that the present
iC^
ICKCU.KWrn. I
•’Ome'ifpm *loc
receive the liberal quantities that will bo
sent in during the winter. If our present
1 stocks of grain could be converted into cash,
it would add largely to the ability of the city
to rebuild, though it is true that a consid
erable proportion of that grain is owned
by parties not resident in Chicago.
It is reported that Mr. S. S. Hayes, the
Democratic candidate for Congressman-at-
Large, has withdrawn from the canvass and
notified the Central Committee of his party
of the fact The grounds stated by him
for this action are understood to be the
pressure of private business requiring his
attention; and the demands upon his rime
by the Relief and Aid Committee.
Who will be selected by the Democratic
Central Committee to take Mr. Hayes*
place on the ticket cannot be guessed, but
already the names of Judge Caton and
Wm. B. Ogden have been mentioned; bat
it is an idle ceremony to make any nomi
nation or selection, as the Republicans are
certain of electing their candidate against
anyone their opponents may put np. The
better way for tbc Democrats to do is to let
that place on the ticket go by default, and
thereby save the candidate the vexation of
defeat.

moans to relievo tbe necessities ol the destitute,

oar, the Chairman. George Schneider, Henry
Oreenebaum, and C. Knobelidorif, tbe bankers.
Consul Clsusseulm, John A. Buck, Alderman
Buebler, Ernst Praising, Peter Hand, Fritz
Metzke. Dr. Ernest Schmidt. Ac.
While the great losses in capital fall on oar
merchant prinees and the property-owners In
the burnt district in the South Side, by far tbe
greater number of losers are Germans: and yet it
is remarkable that but a small proportion of
those applying for passes to leave tho city be
long to that nationality, while, on the contrary,
fully two-tbirds of those applying for lumber to
build temporary dwellings, thus declaring their
Intention to stay here, are Germans. This is as
it should bo: we want these thrifty, intelligent
people here: we must have them; wo cannot get
along without them. And we say to tho gentle
men of the Chiesgo Relief and Aid Society, give
this German Central Committee a fair repre
sentation on year committees and aub-commit
tees; identify them with your system, so that
they may say to their constituents. Wo are a
i part of the syitetn. and know th.U all is well.

SPECIAL COUNSEL AND ASSISTANCE.
The Chicago Relief and Aid Society have
established a Bureau of Special Counsel and
Assistance. Tbo object of this bureau is to give
counsel and assistance to person* whose situation
may require more special consideration than it is
possible to accomplish at the regular offices of
distribution; providing, under proper detailed
organisation, for personal visitation, by lady or
gentleman, as may be requested. Pastors of all
churches of every persuasion arc members of this
bureau. All charitable associations, lodges, and
organizations now existing or horealtcr to bo
organized, having any relation to this work, are
requested to send delegate* to this bureau, who
shall be members of the same, no organization
to send more than one delegate. Tbo meeting
of member* for the purpose of completing it*
organization and plan ol co-operation with ibe
Chicago Relief and Aid Society will bo held at
the First Congregational Church* corne’r of Ann
and Washington streets, West Side,* Monday
afternoon, October 23, at 2 o’clock. All pastors
are requested to give this notice from tho pnlpit
on Sunday next.

THE COMMITTEE ON EMPLOYMENT

SANITARY COMMITTEE.
Dr. H. A. Johnson, from the Committee on
the Sick, Hospitals, and Sanitary Measures, re
ported that an organization was nearly complet
ed for the prompt supply of medical services to
those who may do in need. Sanitary regulations
connected with construction of bouses and with
reference to nlght-idl, drainage matters, etc.,
have been attended to. Many physicians have
volunteered their services and medicines, and
medical stores are furnished as required. The
sanitary condition of the city is good.

Feeding tho Destitute.
Tho work of relieving tbo destitute has now
changed in almost every respect. The amount
»t conked rrovisi -ns sent in t» supply immediate
wants has nearly given cut, and. as a matter of
coarse, the church dinners,at which tbo fair ones
of the flock minister to the wants of the suffer
ers, have ceased. The programme is nnw sys
tematically adhered to. and is so generally ful*
l-w»d that one or two examples will suffice to
give a correct idea of tho machinery of the
society.
A reporter yesterday morning visited the
Plymouth Congregational Cbnrcb. which is un
der the superintendence of Mr. J. C. Hill, who
is assisted by about twenty gentlemen, mainly
volunteer*, and tbo same number of ladies, who
are alto Volunteers. Hero tbs sufferers aro ad
mitted, one at a lime, and registered. They are
then supplied with rations for two days.
In an adjoining room a comber of ladies, vol
unteers. who aro busy all day in dispensing
slothing, too necessity for snob assistance has
already been recognised, an J tbo activity and

tbeir ioqn:rißs. as well as tbeir aptness in de

At the Indiana Avenue Christian Church 2,ißi
persons are registered, but whether any of these

At the Rallied Street Methodist Cburch every
thing appears to be in excellent order, and run
ning smoothly.
The other chnrobcs, where supplies are given
given out. are being run on precisely tbo same
plan, and are more or Icsssuccesstul.
One cannot but be struck, on examining tbe
quality of tbe food given out. at Its excellence.
The finest of flour. tbe best of bread, fresh vege

tea. ail of tho very best. And yet, there are in
stances told of men and women grumbling—ab
solutely rejecting tbe food, a* being fit only lor
those who were starving. These people, in all
human probability, arc enjoying tho luxuries of
life for tbe first time in tbeir lives, hot
ieel constrained to despise what is in every way
too good for them. It is gratifying to learn that
the names of these gambler* were instantly
struck from tbo relief rolls, and iboir owner*
aniotly advised to go elsewhere and get better,
me miserable Irishman observed, atone "i the
churches yesterday, that if they couldn’t give

Storm Warnings.
Tho Signal Service Corps of the United States
army have adopted a plan for signalling tbe ap
proach of dangerous storms, for tho use of marin
ers and others. Tbo signal is usually displayed
fmm the building in which the government
metorologienl observations are taken in (ho sev
rra! cities. The Chicaso station is now located
at No. 10 Vv«-st Randolph street, sod the signal
will probably be displayed from tbo top ofLiod’s
Bl»ck. near Lake street bridge. We append tbe
following explanation! of tb® signal and iu
meanings; The cautionary signal is a redflig
with block square in the centre by day. aod a
red light by night—displayed at die office of tbe
observer, and other prominent places through
out any city. aigoiSes:
1. That from the information bad at tho Cen
tral Office in Washington, a probability of stormy
or dangerous weather has been deduced for tbe
port or place at which the cautionary signal is
displayed, nr in that vicinity.
2. That tbe danger appears to be so great a*
to demand precaution on the part of navigator*
and others interested—such as an examination
ot vessels <>r other structures to bo endangered
by a storm—tbe inspection of crew*, rigging, Ac.,
and general preparation for rough weather.
3- It calls fur trequont oxamioatioo of local
barometers, and other instrnm-nts. by ship cap
tains. or other* interested, aod tbe study of locH
signs of tbe weather, as clouds, etc., etc. By tbis
means those who are expert may often be con
firmed as to tbe need of tho precaution to which
tbo cautionary signal calls attention, or may de
termine that tho danger is over-estimated or
past.
Proclamation by the Slayer.
In view of tbe recent appalling public calam
ity, tbe undersigned, Mayor of Chicago, hereby
earnestly recommend that all tbo inhabitants ol
this city do observe Sunday; October 29. as a
special day of humiliation and prayer: of hu
miliation for tboso past oSVnces a?*iut Almighty
God, i> wbieb these severe afflictions wer*
doubtless intended to.lead our minds; of prayer
lor tbe relief ’and comlort of tbe suffering
thousands in our midst; for the
restoration of que material prosperity,
especially for our lasting improvement as a peo
ple in rcr«reaee_ tmff qbedienoo to God. Nor
sboutd we over, amidst our losses and sorrows,
forget to render thanks t Him for tbo arrest 01
the devouring fires tn time to save so many
homes, and fur tbe unexampled sympathy and
aid which has flawed in upon ns from every
quarter of our land, and even from beyond the
Seas.
Given under my hand this 2ftth day of October,
1871, R. B. Mason, Mayor.

GROUND RENTS FOB THE HOMELESS.
Tho expenditure ot a portion ot the relief
fund iu the erection of temporary h»tnei for the
-belter of those made homeless by the fire is un
questionably one ot the best methodsof extend
ing relief tu tuose who are worthy of it. Many
familieswbose memberjwould otherwise fiodwork
aod support themselves are utterly demoralized,
while the family identity is. in a measure, lost to
•uch promiscuous crowds ot men, women, and
children, as bare found shelter in too churches
and other similar places of reluge. Tbe beads of
families lose their sense uf responsibility
wQen they find each family and each mem
ber of a family on a ootnm-n level in tbe
cmwd of recipients of charity. The object in
giving tbe aid to build these temporary homes
should not be simply to shelter a crowd from tbe
inclemency ol the coming winter, but also to re
establish the families iu bouses that will secure,
m some degree, that privacy necessary to tbe
self-respect ani, consequently, tbo morality of
ibe family. A great many ol the laboring men
and mechanics who will apply for assistance
to erect houses own or control by lease tbe luva
on which tbeir bouses lormerly stood. Tbeie
wi 11, uf course, build their temporary house on
tbe same sites, and will 10 many cases be able to
pay. in tbe course ol six months or a year, for
the material now loaned to them. But there
is a larger class who have no such claim
upon ground anywhere and for whom some loca
tion to build must be pr»vided iu addition to tb®
Into of tbe material to build with. To meet tuis
requirem-nt Mr. Wa> Johnston made the
off r day before yesterday, of tbe uso of 300 lots
on Milwaukee avenue, reut free for three years.
Ibis is a liberal offer which does
that gentleman much credit, and should
prompt other large owners of ground in the sub
urbs to make similar offer*. But if as many as
one or two hundred of tnes® temporary bouses
are to be erected in any one neighborhood, great
care should bs taken tbatthe location is not so
distant Irom work that it would necessarily be
come a mendicant camp instead of a means of
putting tbe sufferers in tbo way of earning tbeir
uva living. It possible, sites should be procur
ed near to tba new manufacturing establish
ments now being erected in various parts of tbe
suburb;, and in wniob tne occupants of the tem
porary booses will find work. Within a radius
of a mile from tbe junction of Renbau
and Twenty-second streets now man
utaeturiog establishments are now
being erected which will famish
permanent employment for at least 2.0U0 men.
and there are probably other as favorable loca
tions. With tbe opportunities that woold bo
offered in these establishments, and alio ia re
building tbe burnt districts, the majority of the
occupants of the temporary bouses would, by
oexc spring, be able to pay back to the relief
mod toe money loaned them to bnild with, aad
would, in many esses, be willing next summer
to enter iqto ccawscti l with the owners
of tbe ground, to pay something for ground rent
after say eighteen* months or two years. Bp ot
tering taeir groqad root free for say two years
<ome of tho large properly owucra may not only
d a groat chanty at once but eventually reap
some benefit trom the establishment «f a per
manent population in districts now entirely un
occupied-

COM.IIEfiCIAL.
Fbidat Evening, Oct. 10.
Business matters exhibited a decided im
provement to-dey, Tho Duurd of Trade is.
probably now more than ever before, an index,
or rather a synopsis of tho outside business
world, and the tone to-day was a cheering one.
Trading in grain was much more active than on
any day since the fire, and confidence in values
seemed to bo re-established. The depression
yesterdav was well marked m the fact that
wheat sold 5c per bushel lower hero than tn Mil
waukee, Tho weakness was almost equally
great in other gram markets, and was duo to
the universal desiro to realize, produced by the
imperative necessity to obtain m‘>ney. Ine
great bulk of the property held by the more
needy ones was sold out yester
day. and buyers to-day wore obliscJ
to pay better prices, thoro being also more pi
them, as pecuniary rugosities are rapidly
becoming smoothed down. Several of oar
most prominent operators had their
checks thrown out by tbo banks
two or threo days ago, but most of them have
made it all rtght.and though money is still scarce
ttnong them, there is no lack of credit of tin
right kind. The only class of business that is
not rapidly recovering is the option trading
among those who are never worth anything. It
would be well it ibis kind of trading never be
resurrected on ’Change. It is probabl-i that tbc
board will ero long give a death blow to the sys
tem by refusing to arbitrate on option deals in j
ojses where no margin has been deposited.
It is gratifying t» note that our shipments are
n tho increase, without a corresponding aug
ment in the receipts of grain. 11 tho receipts
should keen down within moderate compass for
a week or two. they could bo handled without
difficulty, and if tbo shipments keep up, the re
sult will bo to pat our elevators into good con
dition for the winter, and bring, in money with
1 which to repair the waste of the fire,
i Tbo Board of Trado will tomorrow commence
to do business in a more orderly manner, s door
keeper being placed in position to keep out in
truders, Smoking duriug 'Change hours will
also bo prohibited. Hitherto the crush has been
so great owning to the pressure of numerous par
ties who are not members, that
it was almost impossible to move
about in the throng. Another needed reform is
•be establishment of certain locations on the
floor for trading in the different articles of pro
duce. as in the old hull.
There is some Utile difference of opinion
among the stockholders of the Chamber of Com
merce. as to whether it is best to rebuild with
brick and stone trimmings or with stonoas be
fore. Tbo advocates of tho latter course urge
that tho Board of Trade ought to sot tho ax.
